Synchronization and averaging in partially hyperbolic systems with fast and slow variables
Federico Bonetto and Guido Gentile

Abstract
We study a family of dynamical systems obtained by coupling an Anosov map on the two-dimensional torus – the chaotic system – with the identity map on the one-dimensional torus – the neutral system – through a dissipative interaction. We show that the two systems synchronize: the trajectories evolve toward an attracting invariant manifold, and the full dynamics is conjugated to its linearization around the invariant manifold. As a byproduct, we obtain that there exists a unique exponentially mixing physical measure. When the interaction is small, the evolution of the variable which describes the neutral system is very close to the identity; hence, it appears as a slow variable with respect to the variable which describes the chaotic system, and which is wherefore named the fast variable. We demonstrate that, seen on a suitably long time scale, the slow variable effectively follows the solution of a deterministic differential equation obtained by averaging over the fast variable. More precisely, we prove that the invariant manifold is in probability close to the fixed point of the averaged dynamics and that the difference between the exact evolution of the slow variable, seen from the invariant manifold, and its averaged evolution is in probability exponentially decreasing for arbitrarily large times.



1
Introduction

In this paper, we study the long time evolution of a dynamical system obtained by coupling a chaotic
system on the two-dimensional torus with a one-dimensional neutral system
1
1

1

By ‘neutral
system’ we mean a system which in the absence of the interaction is at rest, so that the Lyapunov
exponent of the central direction is zero – and remains close to zero when the interaction is
switched on and is small.
through a weakly dissipative interaction. Our goal is to show the
occurrence of two related phenomena of interest.

The first one is the unfolding of an attracting invariant manifold, on which the dynamics is
essentially a copy of the evolution of the chaotic system. This is what is usually referred to as

synchronization
. In this respect, we extend the results in
[
27
]
to a more
general class of systems and, in addition, we show that in a large neighborhood of the invariant
manifold the dynamics is conjugated to its linearization around the invariant manifold.

The second phenomenon can be described as follows. When the interaction between the two systems is
very small, the evolution of the neutral variable becomes independent of the chaotic variable and is
governed only by the average of the interaction over the chaotic variable.
2
2

2

The ‘chaotic
variable’ and the ‘neutral variable’ are the variables that, in the absence of interaction, describe
the chaotic system and the neutral system, respectively.
More precisely, for an asymptotically full
measure set of initial conditions for the chaotic variable, the evolution of the neutral variable,
on a suitable time scale, is described by a differential equation that does not involve the chaotic
variable. This phenomenon is known as
averaging
and it is studied for systems related to ours
in
[
19
,
20
]
, among others. In the class of systems we consider, we
show that, excluding a set of initial condition whose measure vanishes with the size of the interaction,
the averaged differential equation describes the evolution of the neutral variables
toward the invariant manifold up to an error which vanishes with the interaction and remains
uniformly bounded in time for all times. Moreover, when the dynamics is seen from the invariant
manifold, the averaged differential equation describes the evolution of the neutral variables
with an error exponentially decreasing in time, still after excluding a set of
initial conditions which vanishes with the interaction.

We prove the existence of both the invariant manifold and the conjugation via an explicit
construction relying on the hyperbolic properties of the chaotic system. To obtain averaging we
prove several, progressively more complex correlation inequalities for the chaotic evolution that,
combined with the explicit construction, allow us to control effectively the fluctuations of the
full evolution around the averaged one.

We now give a more detailed description of the results briefly outlined above, without insisting on
the technical aspects. Formal statements are provided in Section
2
, after introducing
all the appropriate notations and definitions.

1.1
Deterministic description: synchronization

Synchronization in quasi-integrable systems is well known to occur in the presence of dissipation; a
typical example is the orbital resonance in celestial mechanics
[
45
]
. By contrast, in chaotic
systems, where trajectories starting at close initial conditions tend to diverge from each other,
synchronization may appear as an unlikely phenomenon. Nonetheless, the presence of negative Lypaunov
exponents due to a dissipative coupling can still produce synchronization
[
47
]
.

One of the simplest models one can think of is the partially hyperbolic system obtained by coupling
a chaotic system, for instance an Anosov automorphism on the two-dimensional torus
\mathds{T}^{2}
, such as
the Arnold’s cat map, with a one-dimensional neutral system on
\mathds{T}
through a dissipative
perturbation which is unidirectional, that is which affects only the motion of the neutral system.
Such a model has been explicitly considered in
[
27
]
, by fully exploiting the perturbative nature
of the coupling and under suitable assumptions which
simplify the analysis to a great extent. More complicated and realistic models can be easily envisaged
[
48
,
49
,
7
,
30
,
3
]
,
however the main advantage of the simple model studied in
[
27
]
is that its solution
can be explicitly worked out and studied in great detail, without resorting to numerical simulations
or heuristic arguments. What is found in
[
27
]
is that a two-dimensional invariant manifold appears on which
the dynamics is conjugated to that of the unperturbed automorphism: as a consequence, the two
systems synchronize asymptotically, in the sense that they tend to realize a drive-response
configuration, with the originally neutral system slaved to follow the dynamics of the chaotic
system (see
[
7
]
and references therein for an introduction to the topics). The
invariant manifold is no more than Hölder continuous, but, with the hypotheses considered in

[
27
]
, its oscillations are small, that is of the same order as the perturbation – a
property which is not expected to hold in general.

In the present paper we study a class of dynamical systems that include those considered in

[
27
]
, and show that an invariant manifold exists in a more general setting, and it is
still the graph of a Hölder continuous function over the two-dimensional torus (see
Theorem
1
). Moreover, we extend the analysis beyond the perturbative regime, by requiring
the coupling only to be dissipative in a finite region. The oscillations of the invariant manifold
may be rather large in general, even in the perturbative regime, albeit large oscillations are rare
in the latter case, given that the variance of the function whose
graph describes the invariant manifold is of the order of the perturbation (see
Theorem
4
).
We also provide a detailed description of the dynamics away from the invariant manifold, by
demonstrating that it is conjugated to its linearization around the invariant manifold (see
Theorem
2
). In particular, the invariant manifold is proved to be an attractor. The
conjugation too, in general, is no more than Hölder continuous.
The variance of the conjugating function, seen as a function on
\mathds{T}^{2}
for fixed value
of the neutral variable, is found to be of the order of the coupling, despite the fact that
deviations of order 1 from its average are possible even in the case of small coupling (see Theorem
5
).
As a byproduct, we obtain that any system in the class we consider admits a unique physical measure given by the
lift to the invariant manifold of the normalised Lebesgue measure of
\mathds{T}^{2}
. This measure is
exponentially mixing, with mixing rate limited by the low regularity of the invariant manifold (see
Theorem
3
).

1.2
Probabilistic description: averaging

When the perturbation is very small, two time scales naturally appear in the evolution: the

fast
time scale of the chaotic dynamics on
\mathds{T}^{2}
as opposed to the
slow
time scale
of the neutral system, whose evolution is driven only by the perturbation. The study of systems with
fast and slow variables is well established for quasi-integrable systems where the motion of the
slow variable is close to periodic, and probably originated with Lagrange’s analysis of the secular
variations of the orbital elements of planets
[
40
]
. In such systems, the
slow variable, for a very long time, only feels the average over one period of its interaction with
the fast variables. Thus, to study the drift of the slow variable, one applies
the so-called
averaging method

[
32
,
5
,
50
]
: once the oscillations of the fast variables have been
integrated out, one finds an approximate solution which provides, in general, a reliable description
of the dynamics up to a time inversely proportional to the slow time scale. However, this
is only the first step in the study of the dynamics of the system, and one has to control the
corrections in a deeper way in order to make the analysis rigorous and extend it up to longer – even infinite – time scales.

In a similar spirit, we investigate the dynamics of the systems we are considering when the
size
\rho
of the perturbation is very small. We find that, in this situation, in order to describe the
evolution of the slow variable, the fast variable, despite being deterministic,
can somehow be treated as a random variable. More precisely, the evolution of
the slow variable becomes essentially independent of the dynamics on the torus and is effectively
described by an
averaged dynamics
in which the function describing the influence of the fast
variable on the slow variable is replaced by its average on the torus. We show that,
taking a random initial condition for the fast
variable, the probability of seeing a sizable deviation from the deterministic averaged evolution is
of the order of the perturbation (see Theorem
6
, complemented by Lemma
2.21
,
and the ensuing Corollary
2.27
). Thanks to the dissipation, which makes the
trajectories to evolve toward the invariant manifold, the probability of the deviations can be
controlled and proved to remain uniformly small along the full trajectory for arbitrarily large
times. Furthermore, for most initial values of the fast variable, the evolution of the slow variable,
when seen from the invariant manifold, is approximated by the averaged evolution
up to corrections decreasing exponentially in time (see Theorem
7
).
We can interpret the above results in the form of a
scaling limit
,
in the sense that we can fix a finite time
t
and study, when the size
\rho
of the perturbation
is very
small, what happens after
\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor
iterations of the map. In this regime,
henceforth called the
scaling regime
, the
averaged dynamics
is described by
a suitable ordinary differential equation.
This implies that the evolution of the slow variable, seen as a continuous time stochastic
process in the rescaled time
t
, converges to the solution of an ordinary differential equation in
probability in the topology of the uniform convergence (see Theorem
8
).

Properties as those discussed above are exhibited by the class of models studied in
[
19
,
21
,
20
,
41
]
,
where the one-dimensional system described by the slow variable is
coupled with an expanding circle map and the coupling is dissipative only in average.
When the system is not uniformly dissipative, the dynamics may become much more complicated.
This is especially true when the averaged system admits more than one attractor.
If this happens, starting close to one of them does not prevent the
dynamics to move toward some other attractor, and, as a consequence, a further
time scale naturally appears, on which
the dynamics appears as a random walk between metastable states.
A result of this kind has been
worked out in the aforementioned models based on expanding maps
[
19
]
,
where the rate of decay of correlations, in the case of more coexisting sinks
for the one-dimensional averaged system, is proved to be much slower than in the
case of only one attractor. An analogous behaviour is expected
to occur also in the case of the Anosov automorphisms, when the interaction is dissipative only in average. On the other hand, in
the fully dissipative case, where synchronization is achieved at exponential
rate, the class of models we consider is more general, because of the
presence of both
stable and unstable directions for the fast chaotic dynamics on
\mathds{T}^{2}
;
furthermore, considering Anosov systems instead of expanding
maps has the advantage that the systems one deals with are reversible and
exhibit symplectic structure
[
41
]
. Noteworthily, the stronger
assumption on the dissipation allows to identify the stable state with the
metastable state (in particular, the last time scale listed in the introduction
of
[
21
]
does not arise), and to obtain stronger bounds for the
decay of the correlations, with no logarithmic corrections.

1.3
More general settings: a brief overview of possible applications

Systems of the kind described above, usually in more complex and articulated variants,
appear in many applications. In fact, the class of systems we study in this paper can be considered as
a prototype – or at least a simplified version – of physically more structured models, in the same spirit as in
[
27
,
20
]
.

One of the first problems to be studied, where fast and slow variables are
coupled to each other, were the planetary motions in celestial mechanics. A
well-known example are the effects of the revolution of the Moon around the
Earth (the fast motion) on the revolution of the Earth around the Sun (the slow motion).
Krylov-Bogolyubov theory provides a useful tool to deal with such a kind of
problems and, more generally, to study the behaviour of oscillating systems where
at least two very different time scales are involved: an averaged equation for
the slow variables is obtained after integrating out the motions of the fast variables
[
37
,
8
,
33
]
.
The theory has been successfully applied to a wide class of dynamical systems,
which range from very simple two-dimensional systems,
such as the Van der Pol equation or the inverted pendulum, to much
more complicated ones, such as the stability of the Solar system, where,
because of the complexity of the equations, numerical analysis plays a dominant role.

Recently the averaging method has been applied to study the stability of the

H_{2}^{+}
ion within the framework of classical mechanics
[
14
]
.
For certain initial conditions of the electron coordinates,
the protons are found numerically to be captured in an oscillatory state.
On the other hand, the numerical simulations also show that for other initial
conditions the motion of the electron becomes chaotic.
It would be interesting to investigate further the chaotic regime, in the light of the
increasing results in the literature showing that synchronization may still occur
when the dynamics of the fast variable moves from regular to chaotic; for
instance, a behaviour of this kind is observed numerically in electromechanical
systems with flexible arms (see
[
38
]
and references quoted therein).
Another recent application of the theory of averaging is provided by the derivation of
Haff’s law for the system formed by two hard disks on a two-dimensional torus
that dissipate energy due to inelastic collisions
[
31
]
: by rewriting
the dynamics in terms of a dispersing billiard, in the limit of
vanishing dissipation, the speed of the particle is approximated uniformly by the solution
of a suitable ordinary differential equation.

Undoubtedly, also in the light of the relation of the problem of climate change
with society and life on our planet,
one of the most significant examples of chaotic systems where fast and slow variables interact
with each other is the Earth’s climate system:
while weather processes, such as the atmospheric and ocean dynamics, behave
as fast motions, what one is really interested in is the slow evolution of
the climate
[
35
]
; moreover, one has to take into account also
intentional and unintentional human-induced perturbations, such as the global
warming due to human activities.
Because of the wide range of external forces
involved in the climate system, the mathematical models which are used to treat
the problem in full generality are inevitably complicated, and the corresponding
differential equations are mainly studied numerically.
However, analytically more accessible models have been studied,
not only because there are problems which admit a simpler description, but also on the grounds that
obtaining analytical results allows us to improve our general understanding of the problem.
A class of simple climate models are the
energy balance models
, where only a few
variables appear. An example is provided by a two-dimensional model where
the evolution of the mean surface temperature and of the mean deep ocean
temperature is governed by a system of two stochastic differential equations:
the climate system response is characterised by two time scales, with the deep
ocean temperature reacting much more slowly
[
52
]
.
The use of mathematical models in order to deal with the climate change has
intensified in the last few years, thanks to the recent developments in
dynamical systems theory as well as in statistical mechanics and probability
(see
[
22
,
42
,
29
]
for reviews on the topics).
The tools we use in the present paper provide a possible path to follow in order
to address the analysis of climate models. Studying coupled Anosov systems,
which in principle could appear as mathematical abstractions, is justified in
consideration of Gallavotti-Cohen chaotic hypothesis
[
28
]
. In this regard,
we stress that the results obtained by relying on Ruelle response theory

[
43
,
29
]
exploit the very same assumption.

Another well established – and more ambitious – line of research aims at deriving rigorously the macroscopic law
of transport of energy in a crystal, that is the
heat equation
, starting from
the deterministic microscopic dynamics (see
[
10
]
for a review).
Since one is interested in the long-time dynamics, in order to avoid the difficulties inherent in perturbations
of integrable Hamiltonian systems one may either add some randomness, which leads to stochastic equations,
or turn to a different approach and move toward strongly chaotic dynamics.
In this perspective, as pointed out in
[
41
]
, one would like to investigate the
case of a large number
N
of chaotic systems, locally coupled with an equal number of neutral systems
and weakly interacting with each other, and look for results
which are uniform in
N
. One is ultimately interested in
taking the
hydrodynamical limit
, that is considering the system
obtained by a scaling limit in which both
N
and the (discrete) time
k
go to infinity
in a suitable way (see
[
53
]
for a review).
A model of this kind, with a different approach with respect to ours, is studied in
[
12
]
,
where the local systems are chaotic maps,
the neutral variables play the role of local energies
and a further conservative small interaction is introduced between the local systems; then,
for initial conditions with the energies confined in a very small region, a
diffusion equation is proved to be satisfied by the local energies at finite time.

Stated in its full generality, the problem is too hard for our present
knowledge. As discussed in
[
19
,
41
]
, a possible strategy to
pursue is to split the study into two separate steps:

1.

First one studies a single local system weakly interacting with a neutral
variable in the limit in which the size
\rho
of the interaction
vanishes (the
scaling regime
). In order to obtain a non-trivial evolution one
studies the behaviour of the system for times
k
diverging with a law which
depends on
\rho
. In this way one obtains a differential equation (the

mesoscopic equation
) describing the dynamics of the neutral variable.

2.

Next, one couples a large number
N
of such systems and takes the limit
in which both
N
and the rescaled time go to infinity according to the
hydrodynamic limit. The heat equation should emerge as the partial differential
equation describing the evolution of the local energy concentration, that is
the average of the neutral variable in a small region.

As far as the first step is concerned, one wishes the dynamics to be well understood in the absence of interaction.
Since integrable systems have to be excluded because they are too special and
are expected to display a non-typical behaviour, the local systems are usually assumed to be chaotic.
A further simplifying hypothesis is to
consider an interaction which makes non-conservative the evolution of the
neutral variable, so that local attractors appear and the dynamics is controlled
over long times. In such a situation the scaling limit
requires
k\sim\rho^{-1}
and the mesoscopic equation is an ordinary
differential equation. In the more difficult conservative case, a different
scaling law
k\sim\rho^{-2}
is looked for and the mesoscopic equation
is expected to be a stochastic differential equation.

The present paper deals with the first step of the strategy outlined above, in the fully dissipative case.
Of course, the problem one has in mind is the general case of partially hyperbolic systems,
where all manifolds – unstable, central and stable – exist,
in the presence of a dissipative interaction along the central direction.
Thus, the fact that the neutral system does not influence the chaotic evolution on the torus,
although it still remains highly non-trivial, makes the analysis easier.
Notwithstanding this simplification, we think that the model we study here
contains most of the relevant features we need to control the statistical properties
over long times of models with more general couplings (see Section
2.5
).
At the same time, it has the advantage of being well suited for explicit computations
and eliminating details which would introduce technical
complications without really adding anything to the underlying physics.
3
3

3

Similar
considerations are at the basis of both the models studied in
[
27
,
16
]

and the skew products given in
[
19
,
20
]
as examples for the general theory in the case of expanding maps.

Therefore, in our opinion, results obtained for such a model represent a first step toward a full mathematical understanding of the problem
of synchronization and averaging in chaotic systems, before considering more realistic situations.

2
Model and Results

In this section, first, we introduce the basic ingredients that will be used in the rest of the paper.
Then, we give the formal definition of the model we study and present our main results,
referring to Sections
3
to
5
– and the Appendices – for the proofs.
A more detailed plan of the paper is given in Subsection
2.6
.

2.1
Basic Ingredients

Set
\mathds{T}:=\mathds{R}/2\pi\mathds{Z}
and consider an Anosov automorphism
A_{0}
on
\mathds{T}^{2}

[
2
,
13
]
, such as Arnold’s cat map
[
4
]
. Call
\lambda_{\pm}
the eigenvalues
and
v_{\pm}
the eigenvectors of
A_{0}
, with
|v_{\pm}|=1
,

|\lambda_{+}|>1>|\lambda_{-}|
and
|\lambda_{-}\lambda_{+}|=1
, and set
\lambda:=|\lambda_{+}|
.

Consider
\Omega:={\mathcal{U}}\times\mathds{T}^{2}
, where
{\mathcal{U}}
is a non-empty closed interval
of either
\mathds{T}
or
\mathds{R}
, and, for any function
f\!:\Omega\to\mathds{R}
and any

\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
, set

\langle f\rangle(\varphi):=\langle f(\varphi,\cdot)\rangle:=\int_{\mathds{T}^{2}}f(\varphi,\psi)\,m_{0}({\rm d}\psi),\qquad\tilde{f}(\varphi,\psi):=f(\varphi,\psi)-\langle f\rangle(\varphi),

(2.1)

where
m_{0}({\rm d}\psi):={\rm d}\psi/(2\pi)^{2}
is the normalised Lebesgue measure on
\mathds{T}^{2}
.

Let

\|f\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{(\varphi,\psi)\in\Omega}|f(\varphi,\psi)|
be the supremum norm on
\Omega

and let
{\mathscr{B}}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
denote the Banach space of the bounded continuous
functions
f
equipped with the norm
\|f\|_{\infty}
. For
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
consider the Hölder seminorm and the two directional Hölder seminorms

|f|_{\alpha}:=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sup_{(\varphi,\psi),(\varphi,\psi^{\prime})\in\Omega}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{|f(\varphi,\psi^{\prime})-f(\varphi,\psi)|}{|\psi^{\prime}-\psi|^{\alpha}},\qquad|f|_{\alpha}^{\pm}:=\!\!\!\!\sup_{(\varphi,\psi)\in\Omega}\sup_{x\in\mathds{R}}\frac{|f(\varphi,\psi+xv_{\pm})-f(\varphi,\psi)|}{|x|^{\alpha}},

(2.2)

that satisfy the inequalities

|f|_{\alpha}^{\pm}\leq|f|_{\alpha}\leq|f|_{\alpha}^{+}+|f|_{\alpha}^{-}
and

\|f\|_{\infty}\leq c_{\alpha}|f|_{\alpha}+\|\langle f\rangle\|_{\infty}
,
with
c_{\alpha}:=(\pi\sqrt{2})^{\alpha}
.
Introduce also the norms

\|f\|_{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}}:=\|f\|_{\infty}+\alpha_{-}|f|_{\alpha_{-}}^{-}+\alpha_{+}|f|_{\alpha_{+}}^{+},\qquad\|f\|_{\alpha}^{-}:=\|f\|_{\alpha,0},\qquad\|f\|_{\alpha}^{+}:=\|f\|_{0,\alpha},

(2.3)

and let
{\mathscr{B}}^{*}_{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}}\!(\Omega,\mathds{R})
,

{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha}^{+}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
and

{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha}^{-}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
denote the anisotropic Banach spaces of the functions
defined on
\Omega
equipped with the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}}
,

\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}^{+}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}^{-}
, respectively. If
\alpha_{-}=\alpha_{+}=\alpha
the norm

\|\cdot\|_{\alpha,\alpha}
is equivalent to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}:=\|\cdot\|_{\infty}+\alpha|\cdot|_{\alpha}

of the
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions, so that

{\mathscr{B}}^{*}_{\alpha,\alpha}(\Omega,\mathds{R})={\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
,
where
{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
is the Banach space defined by the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}
;
on the other hand, using anisotropic Banach spaces allows to treat differently
the stable and unstable manifolds
[
6
,
18
]
, and this will be exploited in what follows.
Finally, observe that
{\mathscr{B}}_{0}(\Omega,\mathds{R})={\mathscr{B}}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
.

It is easy to see that

\displaystyle|fg|_{\alpha}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}|g|_{\alpha}+|f|_{\alpha}\|g\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle|fg|_{\alpha}^{\pm}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}|g|_{\alpha}^{\pm}+|f|_{\alpha}^{\pm}\|g\|_{\infty},

(2.4a)

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{\alpha}\leq\|f\|_{\alpha}\|g\|_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}}\leq\|f\|_{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}}\|g\|_{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}}.

(2.4b)

For
k\geq 1
, define also
{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha,k}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
as the Banach space of
the functions
f\!:\Omega\to\mathds{R}
which are
k
-times continuously
differentiable in the first variable, that is in the variable
\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
, and
such that the first
k-1
derivatives are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous in the
second one, that is in the variable
\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}
, equipped with the norm

|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|f|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k}:=\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\|\partial_{\varphi}^{n}f\|_{\alpha}+\|\partial_{\varphi}^{k}f\|_{\infty}=\sum_{n=0}^{k}\|\partial_{\varphi}^{n}f\|_{\infty}+\alpha\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}|\partial_{\varphi}^{n}f|_{\alpha}\,,

(2.5)

where
\partial_{\varphi}
denotes the derivative with respect to the first variable.
Similarly, define the norms
|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|\cdot|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k}^{+}
and
|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|\cdot|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k}^{-}
,
as in (
2.5
) with
|\cdot|_{\alpha}
replaced with
|\cdot|_{\alpha}^{+}
and
|\cdot|_{\alpha}^{-}
,
and call the respective Banach spaces
{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha,k}^{+}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
and

{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha,k}^{-}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
. As in (
2.4b
) we get

|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|fg|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k}\leq|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|f|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|g|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k},\qquad|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|fg|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k}^{\pm}\leq|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|f|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k}^{\pm}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|g|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha,k}^{\pm}.

(2.6)

We call
{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}_{\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-}}\!(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
the subspace of

{\mathscr{B}}^{*}_{\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-}}\!(\Omega,\mathds{R})

whose functions do not depend on
\varphi
,
and
similarly for
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
,
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\alpha}(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
and

{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
. For such functions, the seminorms
|\cdot|_{\alpha}^{\pm}
and
the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
,
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha_{,}\alpha_{+}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}^{\pm}
are
defined as previously, with the supremum taken over
\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}
only.
The behavior of the seminorms (
2.2
) for
f\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{\alpha}(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
under
the action of
A_{0}^{n}
, for
n\in\mathds{Z}
, is given by

|f{\circ}A_{0}^{n}|_{\alpha}\leq\lambda^{\alpha|n|}|f|_{\alpha}\,,\qquad|f{\circ}A_{0}^{n}|_{\alpha}^{+}\leq\lambda^{\alpha n}|f|_{\alpha}^{+}\,,\qquad|f{\circ}A_{0}^{n}|_{\alpha}^{-}\leq\lambda^{-\alpha n}|f|_{\alpha}^{-}\,.

(2.7)

If the functions
g_{0},\ldots,g_{n-1}
are in
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
, for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
, then

\Bigl|\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}g_{i}{\circ}A_{0}^{\mp i}\Bigr|_{\alpha}^{\pm}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\lambda^{-\alpha i}|g_{i}|_{\alpha}^{\pm}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\not=i\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\|g_{i}\|_{\infty}.\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.8)

Remark 2.1
.

In the following we also consider sets of the form

{\mathcal{A}}=\{(\varphi,\psi):a_{-}(\psi)\leq\varphi\leq a_{+}(\psi)\}
in
\mathds{R}\times\mathds{T}^{2}
,
where
a_{\pm}\!:\mathds{T}^{2}\to\mathds{R}
are Hölder continuous functions.
All the definitions of the norms given above extend naturally if the set

\Omega
is replaced with any other closed subset of
{\mathcal{A}}
as above.
We only need to take the supremum over
(\varphi,\psi)\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
replace (
2.2
) with

|f|_{\alpha}\!:=\!\!\!\!\!\!\sup_{(\varphi,\psi),(\varphi,\psi^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{A}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{|f(\varphi,\psi^{\prime})-f(\varphi,\psi)|}{|\psi^{\prime}-\psi|^{\alpha}},\qquad|f|_{\alpha}^{\pm}\!:=\!\!\!\sup_{(\varphi,\psi)\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sup_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{x\in\RRR}{(\f,\psi+x v_\pm)\in\AAA}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{|f(\varphi,\psi+xv_{\pm})-f(\varphi,\psi)|}{|x|^{\alpha}}.\vskip-2.84544pt

Then, we may define the corresponding Banach spaces in
the same way as before, with
{\mathcal{A}}
instead of
\Omega
.

In order not to introduce further symbols, we use the notation
|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|f|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{0,k}

also to denote the
C^{k}
-norm of any function
f
depending only on the first variable
\varphi
.
We thus identify
C^{k}({\mathcal{D}},\mathds{R})
, for any given subset
{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq\mathds{R}
, with
the subspace of
{\mathscr{B}}_{0,k}({\mathcal{D}}\times\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
of the functions
independent of
\psi
.

A crucial role in our analysis is played by the correlation functions and their
decay due to the hyperbolicity of
A_{0}
.
The following estimate is proved in Appendix
A.2
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let the functions
g_{+}
and
g_{-}
be, respectively, in

{\mathfrak{B}}_{\alpha}^{+}(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
and in
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\alpha}^{-}(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
,
for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
. Then for all
n\in\mathds{N}
one has

\left|\left\langle g_{+}\,g_{-}{\circ}A_{0}^{n}\right\rangle-\langle g_{+}\rangle\langle g_{-}\rangle\right|\leq C_{0}(1+\alpha n)\lambda^{-\alpha n}\|\tilde{g}_{+}\|_{\alpha}^{+}\|\tilde{g}_{-}\|_{\alpha}^{-},\vskip-2.84544pt

for a suitable positive constant
C_{0}
independent of
n
,
\alpha
,
g_{-}
and
g_{+}
.

Remark 2.3
.

Proposition
2.2
implies that, under the same assumptions, for every

\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha
one has

\left|\left\langle g_{+}\,g_{-}{\circ}A_{0}^{n}\right\rangle-\langle g_{+}\rangle\langle g_{-}\rangle\right|\leq C_{0}^{\prime}\lambda^{-\alpha^{\prime}n}\|\tilde{g}_{+}\|_{\alpha}^{+}\|\tilde{g}_{-}\|_{\alpha}^{-}
,
with the positive constant
C_{0}^{\prime}
depending only on
\alpha^{\prime}
.

2.2
The model

We consider the dynamical system defined by the map
{\mathscr{S}}
on

\mathds{T}\!\times\!\mathds{T}^{2}
given by

{\mathscr{S}}(\varphi,\psi)=({\mathscr{S}}_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi),{\mathscr{S}}_{\psi}(\varphi,\psi))=(G(\varphi,\psi),A_{0}\psi),\qquad\qquad G(\varphi,\psi):=\varphi+F(\varphi,\psi)\,,\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.9)

with
F\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{0},6}(\mathds{T}\times\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
, for some
\alpha_{0}\in(0,1]
.
The assumption of Hölder continuity in
\psi
is optimal, inasmuch as
it cannot be weakened and requiring stronger
regularity does not provide stronger results.

Remark 2.4
.

Throughout the paper, for any map
{\mathscr{S}}
, the notation
{\mathscr{S}}^{n}
means
{\mathscr{S}}{\circ}{\mathscr{S}}^{n-1}={\mathscr{S}}{\circ}\ldots{\circ}{\mathscr{S}}
, that is the composition of
{\mathscr{S}}
with itself
n
times.

We assume
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.9
) to satisfy the following further hypotheses.

Hypothesis 1
.

There exists a non-empty closed interval

{\mathcal{U}}\subsetneq\mathds{T}
such that
\partial_{\varphi}{\mathscr{S}}_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)>0
for all
(\varphi,\psi)\in\Omega:={\mathcal{U}}\!\times\!\mathds{T}^{2}
.
If, after a suitable parametrization of
\mathds{T}
, one writes
{\mathcal{U}}=[\phi_{m},\phi_{M}]
, with
\phi_{m}<\phi_{M}
,
then
{\mathscr{S}}_{\varphi}(\phi_{m},\psi)>\phi_{m}
and
{\mathscr{S}}_{\varphi}(\phi_{M},\psi)<\phi_{M}
for all
\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}
.

Hypothesis 2
.

For every
\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}
there is a unique point
S(\psi)\in{\rm int}\,{\mathcal{U}}
such that
F(S(\psi),\psi)=0
.

Set also

S_{m}:=\!\inf_{\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}}\!S(\psi),\quad S_{M}:=\!\sup_{\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}}\!S(\psi),\quad\Lambda:=[S_{m},S_{M}]\times\mathds{T}^{2},\quad\Gamma:=-\!\!\sup_{(\varphi,\psi)\in\Lambda}\!\!\partial_{\varphi}F(\varphi,\psi).

(2.10)

Hypothesis 3
.

One has
\Gamma>0
.

A map
{\mathscr{S}}
satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
defines a partially hyperbolic
system
[
23
]
.By Hypothesis
1
,
{\mathscr{S}}
is injective on
\Omega
and
\Gamma<1
.
By Hypotheses
1
and
2
,
{\mathscr{S}}(\Omega)\subsetneq{\rm int}\,\Omega

and
S(\psi)\in(\phi_{m},\phi_{M})

\forall\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}
, and hence
[S_{m},S_{M}]\subsetneq(\phi_{m},\phi_{M})
.
Hypothesis
3
ensures uniform dissipation.

The following lemma lists a few immediate consequences of Hypotheses
1
–
3
.

Lemma 2.5
.

If
{\mathscr{S}}
satisfies Hypotheses
1
–
3
, then the following
properties hold:

1.

one has
F(\varphi,\psi)>0
for
\varphi<S_{m}
, while
F(\varphi,\psi)<0
for
\varphi>S_{M}
;

2.

one has
\partial_{\varphi}F(\varphi,\psi)>-1
for
(\varphi,\psi)\in\Omega
and hence

-1<\partial_{\varphi}F(\varphi,\psi)\leq-\Gamma
for
(\varphi,\psi)\in\Lambda
;

3.

for any
r>0
there exists
N_{r}\in\mathds{N}
such that

{\mathscr{S}}^{N_{r}}(\Omega)\subset\Lambda_{r}:=[S_{m}-r,S_{M}+r]\times\mathds{T}^{2}
, with

N_{r}\leq\frac{\displaystyle{\max\{\phi_{M}-S_{M},S_{m}-\phi_{m}\}}}{\displaystyle{\inf_{\Omega\backslash\Lambda_{r}}|F(\varphi,\psi)|}}\,;\vskip-5.69046pt

(2.11)

4.

for any
\Gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\Gamma)
there exists
r=r(\Gamma^{\prime})
such that

\partial_{\varphi}F(\varphi,\psi)\leq-\Gamma^{\prime}
for all
(\varphi,\psi)\in\Lambda_{r}
;

5.

the set
\Lambda
is positively invariant under
{\mathscr{S}}
and is attracting for
{\mathscr{S}}
on
\Omega
;

6.

there exists a unique
\overline{\varphi}\in(S_{m},S_{M})

such that
\langle F(\overline{\varphi},\cdot)\rangle=0
.

Remark 2.6
.

Thanks to property 6 in Lemma
2.5
, without loss of generality we choose the
parametrization in Hypothesis
2
in such a way that
\overline{\varphi}=0
.

Remark 2.7
.

By Remark
2.6
,
we can write

F(\varphi,\psi)=\beta(\psi)-\nu(\psi)\,\varphi+\delta(\varphi,\psi)\,\varphi^{2}
,
with
\langle\beta\rangle=0
.
Conversely,
if we replace
F
with the function
F_{0}(\varphi,\psi):=\beta(\psi)-\nu(\psi)\,\varphi+\delta(\psi)\,\varphi^{2}
,
with
\langle\beta\rangle=0
and
4\|\delta\|_{\infty}\|\beta\|_{\infty}<\nu_{0}^{2}
, where we have set

\nu_{0}:=\inf_{\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}}|\nu(\psi)|
,
then it is easy to see that (
2.9
) defined by
F_{0}
instead of
F
satisfies
Hypotheses
1
–
3
with
\phi_{M}=\|\beta\|_{\infty}/2\nu_{0}
and
\phi_{m}=-\phi_{M}
.

Remark 2.8
.

Although
{\mathscr{S}}
is defined as a map on
\mathds{T}\times\mathds{T}^{2}
,
given that
{\mathscr{S}}(\Omega)\subsetneq\Omega
what we are really interested in
only the action of
{\mathscr{S}}
on
\Omega
, that we identify as a subset of
\mathds{R}\times\mathds{T}^{2}
.
For technical reasons, we also need to extend
{\mathscr{S}}|_{\Omega}
to a map
{\mathscr{S}}_{\rm ext}
on

\Omega_{\rm ext}=\mathcal{U}_{\rm ext}\times\mathds{T}^{2}
,
with
{\mathcal{U}}_{\rm ext}\supset{\mathcal{U}}
a closed interval,
in such a way that Hypotheses
1
–
3
remain valid (we refer to Subsection
4.1
for further details).

We call
\varphi
the
slow variable
and
\psi
the
chaotic

or
fast variable
– such
a terminology is motivated by the fact that we are mainly interested in the
limit of small
\Gamma
, where
the neutral variable
\varphi
moves slowly with respect to the chaotic
variable
\psi
describing the hyperbolic system.

2.3
Synchronization

2.3.1
The invariant manifold

If in (
2.9
) we replace
A_{0}
with the
identity
\mathds{1}
, that is if we consider the dynamical system defined by the map

{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathds{1}}(\varphi,\psi)=(\varphi+F(\varphi,\psi),\psi)
, with the function
F
still satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
, then,
setting
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathds{1}}=\{(S(\psi),\psi):\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}\}
, we have

{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathds{1}}({\mathcal{W}}_{\mathds{1}})={\mathcal{W}}_{\mathds{1}}
. It is natural to ask whether a similar
property remains true for
{\mathscr{S}}
notwithstanding the chaotic nature of the
evolution generated by
A_{0}
on
\mathds{T}^{2}
.
We say that
{\mathcal{W}}:=\{(W(\psi),\psi):\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}\}
is an
invariant manifold
for
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.9
)
if we have, for all
\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}
,

{\mathscr{S}}(W(\psi),\psi)=(W(A_{0}\psi),A_{0}\psi).\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.12)

This means that on
{\mathcal{W}}
the dynamics
generated by
{\mathscr{S}}
is conjugated to the dynamics generated by
A_{0}
.

The following result is proved in Subsection
3.1
.

Theorem 1

(
Synchronization
)
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.9
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
.
There exists a unique
invariant manifold
{\mathcal{W}}=\{(W(\psi),\psi):\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}\}\subset\Lambda
for

{\mathscr{S}}\!
, with
W\in{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}_{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}}\!(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
for suitable

\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
.

Remark 2.9
.

In general,
{\mathcal{W}}
is only Hölder continuous in
\psi
even if
F
is smooth in
\psi

and
W
is of order 1 in
\|F\|_{\infty}
even if
F
is
very close to 0, its size depending mainly on
S(\psi)
(see also Remark
3.1
).

2.3.2
The linearized map and the conjugation

To analyze the evolution generated by
{\mathscr{S}}
outside
{\mathcal{W}}
we can try to
conjugate it with its linearization around
{\mathcal{W}}
, that is with the simpler
system
{\mathscr{S}}_{0}
given by

{\mathscr{S}}_{0}(\eta,\psi)=(\kappa(\psi)\eta,A_{0}\psi),\qquad\qquad\kappa(\psi):=1+\partial_{\varphi}F(W(\psi),\psi),\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.13)

where, by Hypothesis
3
, one has
\kappa(\psi)\in(0,1-\Gamma)

for all
\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}
.
In other words, we look for a function
{\mathscr{H}}
– called the
conjugating function
(or simply
conjugation
) – such that

{\mathscr{H}}{\circ}{\mathscr{S}}={\mathscr{S}}_{0}{\circ}{\mathscr{H}}.\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.14)

Remark 2.10
.

Since
{\mathscr{S}}_{0}
is linear in
\eta
, it is easy to see that if
{\mathscr{H}}
is a
solution to (
2.14
) then, for any
a\not=0
, also
{\mathscr{H}}(a\varphi,A_{0}\psi)
is a solution.
Thus, we say that
{\mathscr{H}}
is
the
conjugation if it solves
(
2.14
) and can be written as

{\mathscr{H}}(\varphi,\psi)=({\mathcal{H}}(\varphi,\psi),\psi)
,
with
\partial_{\varphi}{\mathcal{H}}(0,\psi)=1
for every
\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}
.

The following result on the conjugation is proved in Subsection
3.2
.

Theorem 2

(
Conjugation
)
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.9
)
satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
. There exist a set
\Omega_{0}\subset\mathds{R}\times\mathds{T}^{2}
and a function
{\mathscr{H}}\!:\Omega\to\Omega_{0}
that
conjugates
{\mathscr{S}}
via (
2.14
) to
{\mathscr{S}}_{0}
given by (
2.13
).
Moreover
{\mathscr{H}}
can be written as in Remark
2.10
with
{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{*},2}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
,
for a suitable
\alpha_{*}\in(0,\min\{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}\}]
and
\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}
as in Theorem
1
.

Remark 2.11
.

Theorem
2
implies that, for any initial datum in
\Omega
, the evolution generated by
{\mathscr{S}}
leads towards
{\mathcal{W}}
.
Therefore,
{\mathcal{W}}
is a global attractor for the dynamical system
(\Omega,{\mathscr{S}})
.

From the proof of Theorem
2
it is easy to see that the function

{\mathscr{H}}
is invertible; indeed, the following result is proved in Subsection

3.2.4
.

Corollary 2.12
.

The map
{\mathscr{H}}
in Theorem
2
is invertible, and its inverse

{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}\!:\Omega_{0}\to\Omega
, which satisfies

{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}{\circ}{\mathscr{S}}_{0}={\mathscr{S}}{\circ}{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}
,
can be written as
{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(\eta,\psi)=({\mathcal{L}}(\eta,\psi),\psi)
,
with
{\mathcal{L}}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{*},1}(\Omega_{0},\mathds{R})
.

As for
W
, the conjugation and its inverse are no more than
Hölder continuous even if
F
is very smooth in
\psi
.
The proofs of Theorem
2
and of Corollary
2.12

show that
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
can be written as

{\mathcal{H}}(\varphi,\psi)=\varphi-W(\psi)+(\varphi-W(\psi))^{2}h(\varphi,\psi),\qquad{\mathcal{L}}(\eta,\psi)=W(\psi)+\eta+\eta^{2}l(\eta,\psi),

(2.15)

with
h\!\in\!{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{*},1}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
and
l\!\in\!{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{*},1}(\Omega_{0},\mathds{R})
.
These representations are used in Subsection
2.4.4
,
when studying the deviations of the conjugation.

2.3.3
The physical measure

Theorems
1
and
2
imply that, given any observable

{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha,0}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
, one has

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}{\mathcal{O}}({\mathscr{S}}^{n}(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0}))=\int{\mathcal{O}}(W(\psi),\psi)\,m_{0}({\rm d}\psi)=:\nu_{W}({\mathcal{O}})\vskip-2.84544pt

for almost every
(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0})\in\Omega
with respect to the measure

\nu_{0}({\rm d}\varphi{\rm d}\psi):=m_{0}({\rm d}\psi){\rm d}\varphi/(\phi_{M}-\phi_{m}).

Hence,
\nu_{W}
is the unique
physical measure
for
{\mathscr{S}}
on
\Omega
. It
is thus interesting to study the mixing property of
\nu_{W}
with respect to
\nu_{0}
.
The following result is proved in Subsection
3.3
.

Theorem 3

(
Mixing on the invariant manifold
)
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.9
)
satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
. For any observables
{\mathcal{O}}_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{*},1}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
,
with
\alpha_{*}
as in Theorem
2
, and any
{\lambda_{*}^{-1}}>\max\{\lambda^{-\alpha_{*}},{1-\Gamma}\}
, one has

\left|\nu_{0}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{1}\,{\mathcal{O}}_{2}{\circ}{\mathscr{S}}^{n}\right)-\nu_{0}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{1}\right)\nu_{W}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right)\right|\leq C_{1}\,{|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\mathcal{O}}_{1}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha_{*},0}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\mathcal{O}}_{2}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha_{*},1}}{\lambda_{*}^{-n}},\vskip-2.84544pt

where the constant
C_{1}
depends only on
\lambda_{*}
.

2.4
Averaging

We are interested in the long time evolution generated by the map
{\mathscr{S}}\!
in (
2.9
),
when the component
{\mathscr{S}}_{\varphi}
is close to the identity.
To this aim, we consider the family of functions
F(\varphi,\psi)=\rho f(\varphi,\psi)
,
with
f\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{0},6}(\mathds{T}\times\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
and
\rho>0
a parameter,
and study the behaviour of the map

{\mathscr{S}}(\varphi,\psi)=(\varphi+\rho f(\varphi,\psi),A_{0}\psi)\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.16)

when
\rho
is small.
We also define

\gamma:=-\sup_{(\varphi,\psi)\in\Lambda}\partial_{\varphi}f(\varphi,\psi)
,
so that
\Gamma=\rho\,\gamma
in (
2.10
).

Remark 2.13
.

We may and do assume, without loss of generality, that
|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|f|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha_{0},6}=1
,
and hence, since
\gamma<|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|f|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha_{0},6}
, that
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
Hypothesis
1
requires
\rho
not to be arbitrarily large.
Therefore, when considering a map
{\mathscr{S}}
of the form (
2.16
), we tacitly assume
\rho
to be smaller than
a suitable value
\rho_{*}
, depending on
f
, such that
{\mathscr{S}}
satisfies
Hypotheses
1
–
3
for all
\rho\in(0,\rho_{*})
.

We investigate the evolution generated by
{\mathscr{S}}
, as given in (
2.16
), when
\rho\to 0^{+}
. In such a limit the evolution tends to become trivial. For this reason, for given initial
conditions
(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0})
, one usually studies the dynamics after a linear
rescaling
of
time, that is one considers
the trajectory
{\mathscr{S}}^{n}(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0})
, with
n=\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor
, where
t
is fixed and
\rho
tends to
0^{+}
.
In the remaining part of this section
we present results for the invariant manifold and the conjugation and
discuss properties
of
{\mathscr{S}}^{n}
which are uniform in
n
, when
\rho
is small
(see Subsections
2.4.2
to
2.4.5
); finally,
we interpret the results in terms of the scaling limit (see Subsection
2.4.6
).
Throughout the paper, we refer to the regime where
\rho
is small as the
scaling regime

even when we look for properties which do not involve the rescaled variable
t
.

2.4.1
Heuristic discussion

If
\rho
is small enough we can take
n
very large but still much smaller than

{\rho^{-1}}
. Expanding
{\mathscr{S}}^{n}
to first order in
\rho
, we write

({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)=\varphi+\rho\sum_{i=0}^{n}f(\varphi,A_{0}^{i}\psi)+o(n\rho).

Since
A_{0}
is strongly mixing, we further obtain that

({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)\simeq\varphi+n\rho\,\langle f\rangle(\varphi)+o(n\rho)

for most values of
\psi_{0}
,
where the right hand side has lost any dependence on
\psi
, at least at first
order in
\rho
. Calling
n\rho=t
and writing
\varphi(t):={\mathscr{S}}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor}(\varphi,\psi)
, we can read the last expression as

\varphi(t)\simeq\varphi+\langle f\rangle(\varphi)\,t+o(t)
.
This propounds that, for
\rho
small, the evolution
\varphi(t)
that starts from a given
\varphi
and a randomly chosen
\psi
is
essentially independent of
\psi
and agrees at first order in
t
with the solution
\phi(t)
of the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\dot{\phi}=\langle f\rangle(\phi)\\
&\phi(0)=\varphi\,.\end{aligned}\right.\vskip-5.69046pt

(2.17)

To see whether we can get a better agreement, we expand
{\mathscr{S}}^{n}
to second order in
\rho
and find

({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)=\varphi+\rho\sum_{i=0}^{n}f(\varphi,A_{0}^{i}\psi)+\rho^{2}\!\!\!\!\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq n}\!\!\!\partial_{\varphi}f(\varphi,A_{0}^{j}\psi)f(\varphi,A_{0}^{i}\psi)+{o(n^{2}\rho^{2})}\,,\vskip-5.69046pt

so that, for
\varphi(t)
to remain close to
\phi(t)
up to corrections
o(t^{2})
, we need that

\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq n}\partial_{\varphi}f(\varphi,A_{0}^{j}\psi)f(\varphi_{0},A_{0}^{i}\psi)\simeq\frac{n^{2}}{2}\partial_{\varphi}\langle f\rangle(\varphi)\langle f\rangle(\varphi),

that is we need a strong form of decay of correlations for
A_{0}
.
This provides a simple instance of the idea of averaging induced by the chaotic behavior of
A_{0}
,
as it suggests that, on the correct time scale,
({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)

evolves according to a differential equation involving only the average of
f
.

Clearly, the argument above is only heuristic. In fact, extending the analysis
outlined above to all orders is likely to get too tangled and to require very
high regularity of the map. Moreover such an analysis is not suitable for
dealing with the case of arbitrary
t
, in particular for deriving results
uniform in
t
. Therefore, the heuristic argument only hints what to look for,
but, in order to obtain something rigorous, actually we follow a different approach.

2.4.2
Synchronization in the scaling regime

The following two lemmas collect the implications of Theorems
1
and

2
and their proofs for the dynamical system
{\mathscr{S}}
in
(
2.16
) with
\rho
small – for the proofs see the end of Subsections
3.1

and
3.2.3
, respectively.

Corollary 2.14
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
.
Let
{\mathcal{W}}=\{(W(\psi),\psi):\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}\}

be the invariant manifold for
{\mathscr{S}}
as in Theorem
1
.
One has
\alpha_{+}=O(1)
and
\alpha_{-}=O(\rho)
, and, furthermore,
both
\|W\|_{\infty}
and
|W|_{\alpha_{-}}^{-}
are
O(1)
, while
|W|_{\alpha_{+}}^{+}=O(\rho)
.

Corollary 2.15
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
.
Let
{\mathscr{H}}
be the conjugation in Remark
2.10
for
{\mathscr{S}}
as in Theorem
2
,
and let
h
and
l
be defined as in (
2.15
).
One has
\alpha_{*}=O(\rho)
, while both
|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|h|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha_{*},1}
and
|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|l|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha_{*},1}

are
O(1)
in
\rho
.

Corollary
2.14
shows that, when
\rho
is small, the invariant manifold
{\mathcal{W}}

loses most of the smoothness of
f
in the stable direction while maintaining it and varying slowly in the unstable one.

By using Corollary
2.14
, the following corollary follows immediately from Theorem
3
.

Corollary 2.16
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
.
For any observables
{\mathcal{O}}_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{*},1}
, with
\alpha_{*}
as
in Theorem
2
, and for all
n\in\mathds{N}
, one has

\left|\nu_{0}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{1}\,{\mathcal{O}}_{2}{\circ}{\mathscr{S}}^{n}\right)-\nu_{0}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{1}\right)\nu_{W}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right)\right|\leq C_{2}\,{|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\mathcal{O}}_{1}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha_{*},0}}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\mathcal{O}}_{2}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{\alpha_{*},1}e^{-\xi\rho n},\vskip-2.84544pt

where
C_{2}
and
\xi
are suitable constants not depending on
\rho
.

2.4.3
The averaged map

As an intermediate step toward a rigorous justification of the conclusions drawn in Subsection
2.4.1
,
we consider also the dynamical system described by the
averaged map

\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}(\varphi,\psi):=(\overline{G}(\varphi),A_{0}\psi)\,,\qquad\qquad\overline{G}(\varphi):=\varphi+\rho\overline{f}(\varphi),\qquad\qquad\overline{f}(\varphi):=\langle f\rangle(\varphi),\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.18)

and its linearization

\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{0}(\varphi,\psi):=(\overline{\mu}\,\varphi,A_{0}\psi),\qquad\qquad\overline{\mu}:=\partial_{\varphi}\overline{G}(0)=1+\rho\,\partial_{\varphi}\overline{f}(0).\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.19)

Noting that

\overline{\Gamma}=\rho\,\overline{\gamma}:=-\rho\sup_{\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}}\partial_{\varphi}\overline{f}(\varphi)\geq\rho\,\gamma=\Gamma
,
we see that
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
satisfies Hypotheses
1
–
3
if
{\mathscr{S}}
does.

Remark 2.17
.

Since
\overline{f}(0)=0
(see Remark
2.6
), we see that
\overline{W}(\psi)=0

solves the equation
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}(\overline{W}(\psi),\psi)

=(\overline{W}(A_{0}\psi),A_{0}\psi)
,
and hence
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
admits the invariant manifold
\overline{{\mathcal{W}}}=\{(0,\psi),\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}\}
.

Remark 2.18
.

Although the action of
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
on each variable is independent of the other one, since one has
(\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)\!=\!\overline{G}^{n}(\varphi)
,
the notation introduced in (
2.18
) helps clarify the forthcoming discussion.

In Subsections
2.4.4
and
2.4.5

we compare the evolution generated by

{\mathscr{S}}
with the evolution generated by
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
. In the remainder of
this subsection, we show that, by adapting the analysis in Subsection
2.3.2
to

\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
, the trajectories of the dynamics generated by

\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
can be compared to the solutions of (
2.17
).

First, we proceed as in Theorem
2
, and look for an invertible function
\overline{{\mathscr{H}}}
such that

\overline{{\mathscr{H}}}{\circ}\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}=\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{0}{\circ}\overline{{\mathscr{H}}}\,,\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.20)

that is a function
\overline{{\mathscr{H}}}
that conjugates
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
to
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{0}
. We write

\overline{{\mathscr{H}}}(\varphi,\psi)=(\overline{{\mathcal{H}}}(\varphi),\psi),\qquad\overline{{\mathcal{H}}}(\varphi)=\varphi+\varphi^{2}\overline{h}(\varphi),\qquad\overline{{\mathscr{H}}}^{-1}(\eta,\psi)=(\eta+\eta^{2}\bar{l}(\eta),\psi).\vskip-5.69046pt

(2.21)

The following result is proved in Subsection
4.6
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.9
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
,
and define
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
as in (
2.18
).
There exist a closed interval
{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\subset\mathds{T}
and a function

\overline{{\mathscr{H}}}\!:{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathds{T}^{2}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times\mathds{T}^{2}
such that (
2.20
) holds.
Moreover, there exists a constant
C_{3}
such that one has

|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|\overline{h}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{0,3}\leq C_{3}
and
|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|\bar{l}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|{\hskip-0.85355pt}|_{0,3}\leq C_{3}
.

Remark 2.20
.

Note that
\Omega_{0}\neq{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times\mathds{T}^{2}
: in fact, one has
\Omega_{0}={\mathscr{H}}(\Omega)
,
while
{\mathcal{U}}_{0}=\overline{{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathcal{U}})
.

Let
\Phi
be the flow generated by (
2.17
), that is the set of the solutions of

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}(\varphi)=\overline{f}(\Phi_{t}(\varphi)),\\
&\Phi_{0}(\varphi)=\varphi,\end{aligned}\right.\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.22)

when varying
\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
. Because of Hypotheses
1
–
3
,
all trajectories
\Phi_{t}(\varphi)
of the system (
2.22
), with
\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
,
move towards the origin at exponential rate.

The following result, proved in Subsection
4.7
, shows
that the trajectories generated by (
2.18
) and (
2.22
) remain close and, in fact, merge asymptotically.

Lemma 2.21
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
,
and define the map
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
as in (
2.18
) and the flow
\Phi
as in (
2.22
).
For any
\gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\gamma)
there exists a positive constant
C_{4}

such that for all
\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
and all
n>0
one has

|(\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)-\Phi_{n\rho}(\varphi)|\leq C_{4}\rho\,(1-\rho\gamma^{\prime})^{n}.

(2.23)

Remark 2.22
.

With the notation established after Remark
2.13
, when introducing the scaling terminology,
we can write (
2.23
) as

|(\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}{\vphantom{s}}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)-\Phi_{t}(\varphi)|\leq C_{4}^{\prime}\rho\,e^{-\gamma^{\prime}t}
,
for a suitable constant
C_{4}^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.23
.

The decay rate in Lemma
2.21
is not in general equal to
\gamma
as one could naïvely
expect. Indeed, the constant
C_{4}
in Lemma
2.21
, as well as the constants
C_{6}
,

C_{8}
and
C_{10}
in the forthcoming Lemma
2.25
, Theorem
6
and
Theorem
7
, respectively, depend on
\gamma^{\prime}
and may diverge as
\gamma^{\prime}
tends to
\gamma
.

2.4.4
Oscillations and deviations in the scaling regime

In the next subsection we show that
({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}
remains close to
(\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}^{n})_{\varphi}
and hence to
\Phi_{n\rho}
,
in the sense that the first and second moments, with respect to
\psi
, of their difference are
O(\rho)

uniformly in
n
. In this subsection we present several preparatory results
that are of interest in their own. The proofs of these
results form the main technical part of the present work and are reported in
Section
4
. The main tools used in the proofs are the decay of
correlations estimates provided by Propositions
4.3
and
4.24
.

We first show that even though the oscillations of
{\mathcal{W}}
around

\overline{{\mathcal{W}}}
can be of order 1 in
\rho
, large oscillations are rare,
in the sense of the following result, proved in Subsection
4.3
.

Theorem 4

(
Oscillations of the invariant manifold
)
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in
(
2.16
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
. Let

{\mathcal{W}}=\{(W(\psi),\psi):\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}\}
be the invariant manifold for the map

{\mathscr{S}}
as in Theorem
1
.
There is a constant
C_{5}
such that

\left|\langle W\rangle\right|\leq C_{5}\rho,\qquad\qquad\langle W^{2}\rangle\leq C_{5}\rho.

(2.24)

From Theorem
4
and Chebyshev inequality we obtain that, for any
\delta>0
,

m_{0}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}:|W(\psi)|>\delta\Bigr\}\Bigr)\leq\frac{C_{5}\rho}{\delta^{2}}.\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.25)

Therefore, the invariant manifold
{\mathcal{W}}
for
{\mathscr{S}}
converges in probability
to the invariant manifold
\overline{{\mathcal{W}}}
of
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
.

Next we compare the linearized maps
{\mathscr{S}}_{0}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{0}
, defined in
(
2.13
) and (
2.19
), respectively. Observe that

\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{0}^{n}(\varphi,\psi)=(\overline{\mu}^{n}\varphi,A_{0}^{n}\psi)
,
while
{\mathscr{S}}_{0}^{n}(\varphi,\psi)=(\kappa^{(n)}(\psi)\varphi,A_{0}^{n}\psi)
, where

\kappa^{(n)}(\psi):=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\kappa(A_{0}^{i}\psi)\,,\vskip-8.5359pt

(2.26)

with
\kappa(\psi)
defined in (
2.13
).

Remark 2.24
.

Throughout the paper we use the convention that a product
over an empty
set of indices is 1,
while a sum over an empty set of indices is 0. In particular, this means that
\kappa^{(0)}=1
in (
2.26
).

The following result, proved in Subsection
4.4
,
shows that the linearized maps
{\mathscr{S}}_{0}^{n}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{0}^{n}
stay close to each other uniformly in
n
;
what makes the result not trivial is that the function
\kappa
in (
2.26
)
is only in
{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}_{\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}}(\mathds{T}^{2},\mathds{R})
,
with
\alpha_{-}=O(\rho)
and
|W|^{-}_{\alpha_{-}}=O(1)
in
\rho
.

Lemma 2.25
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
)
satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
.
Let
\kappa^{(n)}(\psi)
and
\overline{\mu}
be defined
in (
2.26
) and in (
2.19
), respectively.
Then for any
\gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\gamma)
there is a constant
C_{6}
such that, for all
n\in\mathds{N}
,

\bigl|\langle\kappa^{(n)}-\overline{\mu}^{n}\rangle\bigr|\leq C_{6}\rho\,(1-\rho\,\gamma^{\prime})^{n},\qquad\bigl|\langle\bigl(\kappa^{(n)}-\overline{\mu}^{n}\bigr)^{2}\rangle\bigr|\leq C_{6}\rho\,(1-\rho\gamma^{\prime})^{2n}.\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.27)

Finally, we estimate the deviation of
{\mathcal{H}}
from
\overline{{\mathcal{H}}}
. To this end,
it is enough to study the deviations of
h
from
\overline{h}
. The following result is proved in Subsection
4.8
.

Theorem 5

(
Deviations of the conjugation
)
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
.
Let
h
and
\overline{h}
be defined as in (
2.15
) and in (
2.21
), respectively.
There is a constant
C_{7}
such that, for all
\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle h(\varphi,\cdot)-\overline{h}(\varphi)\right\rangle\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{7}\rho,

\displaystyle\left\langle(h(\varphi,\cdot)-\overline{h}(\varphi))^{2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C_{7}\rho,

(2.28a)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\partial_{\varphi}h(\varphi,\cdot)-\partial_{\varphi}\overline{h}(\varphi)\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{7}\rho,

\displaystyle\left\langle(\partial_{\varphi}h(\varphi,\cdot)-\partial_{\varphi}\overline{h}(\varphi))^{2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C_{7}\rho.

(2.28b)

Bounds analogous to (
2.28
) hold for the deviations of

l
from
\overline{l}
, with
l
and
\overline{l}
also defined in (
2.15
) and (
2.21
), respectively;
we refer to Subsection
4.8
– to Proposition
4.49
in particular – for a precise statement,
which requires introducing a suitable extension of the map
{\mathscr{S}}
along the lines outlined in Remark
2.8
.
Note that it is in order to prove the bounds in Theorem
5
and in Proposition
4.49
that we need
F
to be in
{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{0},6}(\Omega,\mathds{R})
(see also Remark
4.44
).

2.4.5
Summing up: convergence in square mean and in probability

We can now complete the comparison of the evolution generated by
{\mathscr{S}}
with
that generated by
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
. From (
2.14
) and (
2.20
) we get

\big({\mathscr{S}}^{n}\big)_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)-(\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)={\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\bigl({\mathscr{S}}_{0}^{n}({\mathcal{H}}(\varphi,\psi),\psi)\bigr)-\overline{{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}\bigl(\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}_{0}^{n}(\overline{{\mathcal{H}}}(\varphi),\psi)\bigr).\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.29)

Combining the estimates in Lemma
2.25
with the bounds in Theorem
5

and the analogous bounds for the inverse conjugation in Proposition
4.49
,
the following result is proved in Subsection
4.10
.

Theorem 6

(
Convergence in square mean
)
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
)
satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
. For any
\gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\gamma)

there exists a constant
C_{8}
such that, for all
n\in\mathds{N}
and all
\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl\langle({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\cdot)-\bigl((\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\cdot)+W(A_{0}^{n}\cdot)\bigr)\bigr\rangle\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq C_{8}\rho\,(1-\rho\,\gamma^{\prime})^{n},

(2.30a)

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\bigl(({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\cdot)-\bigl((\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\cdot)+W(A_{0}^{n}\cdot)\bigr)\bigr)^{\!2}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C_{8}\rho\,(1-\rho\,\gamma^{\prime})^{2n}.

(2.30b)

Remark 2.26
.

The bound (
2.30b
) for the second moment of the fluctuations and the Cauchy-Schwartz
inequality trivially imply a weaker bound than (
2.30a
). By contrast, proving that also the
first moment of the fluctuations is of order
\rho
requires a substantially greater amount of work.
Apart of its intrinsic interest, the stronger bound (
2.30a
)
plays a crucial role in view of some of the extensions proposed in Section
2.5
.
A similar comment holds for the results in
Theorems
4
and
5
, and in Lemma
2.25
as well.

The following result is an immediate consequence of Theorems

4
and
6
, and of Lemma
2.21
.

Corollary 2.27
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
6
, there exists a constant
C_{9}

such that, for all
n\in\mathds{N}
and all
\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
, one has

\bigl|\bigl\langle({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\cdot)-\Phi_{n\rho}(\varphi)\bigr\rangle\bigr|\leq C_{9}\rho,\qquad\qquad\bigl\langle\bigl(({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\cdot)-\Phi_{n\rho}(\varphi)\bigr)^{2}\bigr\rangle\leq C_{9}\rho,

(2.31)

with the flow
\Phi
defined as in (
2.22
).

From Theorem
6
,
for fixed
\varphi
and
n
, we obtain that,
for any
\gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\gamma)
and for any
\delta>0
,

m_{0}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}:(1-\rho\,\gamma^{\prime})^{-n}\bigl|({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)-\bigl(\Phi_{n\rho}(\varphi)+W(A_{0}^{n}\psi)\bigr)\bigr|>\delta\Bigr\}\Bigr)\leq\frac{C_{9}\rho}{\delta^{2}},\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.32)

with the constant
C_{9}
as in (
2.30
).
Note that the set of angles
\psi
considered in (
2.32
) depends on
n
,
even though its measure is bounded independently of the value of
n
.

The following theorem,
proved in Subsection
5.1
,
shows that, for most most values of
\psi
, the difference
between
({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)
and
\Phi_{n\rho}(\varphi)+W(A_{0}^{n}\psi)
is small, exponentially in
n
.

Theorem 7

(
Convergence in probability, I
)
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
)
satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
. Let the flow
\Phi
be defined
as in (
2.22
). For any
\gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\gamma)
there exists a constant
C_{10}
such that, for all
\varphi\in{\mathcal{U}}
,

m_{0}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}:\sup_{n\geq 0}\,(1-\rho\,\gamma^{\prime})^{-n}\bigl|({\mathscr{S}}^{n})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)-\bigl(\Phi_{n\rho}(\varphi)+W(A_{0}^{n}\psi)\bigr)\bigr|>\delta\Bigr\}\Bigr)\leq\frac{C_{10}\rho}{\delta^{3}}.\vskip-5.69046pt

(2.33)

In (
2.33
) we can chose
\delta
as a function of
\rho
in such a way that both
\delta
and
\rho/\delta^{3}
tend to 0
as
\rho
tends to 0 (a natural choice is
\delta=\rho^{1/4}
so that also
\rho/\delta^{3}=\rho^{1/4}
).
Thus,
Theorem
7
implies that, up to a set of initial conditions for
\psi
whose measure
vanishes with
\rho
, the difference between the actual evolution of the neutral variable
toward the invariant manifold and its approximate evolution as described by the averaged
differential equation vanishes with
\rho
as well. Moreover, such a difference remains
uniformly bounded in time for arbitrarily long times.

2.4.6
Aftermath: continuous time

We now give a more probabilistic description
of the results in Theorem
7
on the relations between

{\mathscr{S}}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor}
and the solutions of
(
2.17
) and express them in terms of the rescaled time
t
. To this aim,
for every
\varphi\in\mathds{T}
and every
t\in\mathds{R}^{+}
, we
consider the random variable
X_{t}\!:\mathds{T}^{2}\to\mathds{T}
defined as

X_{t}(\psi):=({\mathscr{S}}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)+(t/\rho-\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor)\bigl(({\mathscr{S}}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor+1})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)-({\mathscr{S}}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor})_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)\bigr).\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.34)

For any
\psi
,
X_{t}(\psi)
is a continuous function of
t
, so that
X_{t}
can be seen as a
stochastic process with trajectories in
C^{0}(\mathds{R}^{+},\mathds{T})
.
We consider also the process
{\mathcal{X}}_{t}
,
with trajectories in
C^{0}(\mathds{R}^{+},\mathds{T})
, given by

{\mathcal{X}}_{t}(\psi)=X_{t}(\psi)-W(A_{0}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor}\psi)-(t/\rho-\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor)\bigl(W(A_{0}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor+1}\psi)-W(A_{0}^{\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor}\psi)\bigr).\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.35)

We want to compare the stochastic processes
X_{t}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{t}
with the
flow
\Phi_{t}
defined in (
2.22
), seen as a stochastic process on

C^{0}(\mathds{R}^{+},\mathds{T})
.
To this aim we introduce the norm

\|x\|^{\rm exp}_{\xi}:=\sup_{t\geq 0}\,e^{\xi t}|x(t)|
,
with
\xi\geq 0
. Combining Lemma
2.21
and Theorem
7
gives
the following result, proved in Subsection
5.2
.

Theorem 8

(
Convergence in probability, II
)
.

Consider the dynamical system described by
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.16
) satisfying Hypotheses
1
–
3
.
Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{t}
and
\Phi_{t}
the stochastic process (
2.35
) and the flow
defined in (
2.22
), respectively. One has

\lim_{\rho\to 0^{+}}{\mathcal{X}}_{t}=\Phi_{t}
.
where the limit is taken in probability in the topology on

C^{0}(\mathds{R}^{+},\mathds{T})
generated by the norm
\|\cdot\|^{\rm exp}_{\xi}

for any
\xi\!\in\![0,\gamma)
.

We close our results with an immediate consequence of Theorem
8
.

Corollary 2.28
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
8
and let

X_{t}
be the stochastic process defined in (
2.34
). One has

\lim_{\rho\to 0^{+}}X_{t}=\Phi_{t}
,
where the limit is taken in probability in the topology of the uniform convergence in
C^{0}(\mathds{R}^{+},\mathds{T})
.

2.5
Extensions and generalizations

Systems of the form (
2.9
) are called
skew products
since the
fast variable does not depend on the slow variable.
Skew product systems are widely studied in the literature, with a view to focus on the
major features of the equations and discard the more technical aspects;
for averaging in fast-slow systems where the base is an expanding map we may quote
[
39
]
,
beyond the illustrative examples proposed in
[
19
,
20
]
.
Here, we sketch a path to generalize our results to the case of a fully coupled system.

As a first step one may consider systems of the form

{\mathscr{S}}(\varphi,\psi)=(\varphi+F_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi),{\mathcal{A}}(\psi)),\qquad{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\psi):=A_{0}\psi+F_{\psi}(\psi),\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.36)

with
F_{\psi}\!:\mathds{T}^{2}\to\mathds{T}^{2}
and
F_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)\!:\mathds{T}\times\mathds{T}^{2}\to\mathds{T}
such that
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\psi)

describes an Anosov diffeomorphism on
\mathds{T}^{2}
and
{\mathscr{S}}
still satisfies
Hypotheses
1
–
3
. However, since any Anosov diffeomorphism of the form in (
2.36
)
is conjugated with its linear part
A_{0}
(see also
[
9
,
25
,
46
]
for a more general context),
there exists a Hölder continuous map

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\!:\mathds{T}^{2}\to\mathds{T}^{2}
such that

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}{\circ}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}=A_{0}{\circ}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
,
so that, setting
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}(\varphi,\psi):=(\varphi,{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(\psi))
,
we may use
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}
to conjugate
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.36
) with

{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}(\varphi,\psi)=(\varphi+F^{\prime}(\varphi,\psi),A_{0}\psi),\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.37)

with
F^{\prime}(\varphi,{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(\psi))=F_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)
.
Clearly
{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}
is of the form (
2.9
) and satisfies Hypotheses
1
–
3
.
In this situation it is still natural to chose the initial
\psi
distributed
according to the Lebesgue measure
m_{0}
on
\mathds{T}^{2}
. This is essentially equivalent to
considering the SRB measure
m_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}
associated with
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
, since
({\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{*})^{n}m_{0}
converges
exponentially fast to
m_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}

[
26
]
.
Thus, to apply the results in Section
2
to
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
2.36
),
one needs estimates on the decay of correlations like those in Proposition
2.2

but with
({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})^{*}m_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}
in place of
m_{0}
.
Observe that
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
is Hölder continuous and
({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})^{*}m_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}
is
invariant under the action of
A_{0}
; thus
m_{{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}
can be represented as a Gibbs
state on the same subshift of finite type used for
A_{0}
in Appendix

A.1
. One thus need to extend the proof Proposition
2.2
using the properties
of the potential that generates such a Gibbs state as discussed in
[
26
]
.

The next step is to look at systems with bidirectional perturbations of the form

{\mathscr{S}}(\varphi,\psi)=(\varphi+F_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi),\mathcal{A}(\psi;\varphi)),\qquad\mathcal{A}(\psi;\varphi):=A_{0}\psi+F_{\psi}(\varphi,\psi)),\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.38)

with
F_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)
satisfying once more Hypotheses
1
–
3
and
F_{\psi}(\varphi,\psi)

such that the
dynamical system on
\mathds{T}^{2}
generated by the map
\mathcal{A}(\cdot;\varphi)

is an Anosov diffeomorphism for any fixed
\varphi\in[\phi_{m},\phi_{M}]
.
An invariant manifold is proved to exist for the system (
2.38
) in
[
16
]
in
the perturbative regime under stronger conditions.
More generally, one looks for a conjugation

{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}(\varphi,\psi)=(\varphi,{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(\varphi,\psi))
such that

{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}{\circ}{\mathscr{S}}={\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}{\circ}{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}
,
with
{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}
of the form (
2.37
). This means that one has

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(\varphi+F_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi),{\mathcal{A}}(\psi;\varphi))=A_{0}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(\varphi,\psi),\qquad\qquad F^{\prime}(\varphi,{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(\varphi,\psi))=F(\varphi,\psi).\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.39)

It is possible to write a formal solution to (
2.39
) (see also
[
11
]
for a similar argument). Applying the conjugation
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}

to the system (
2.38
) allows us to reduce it to a system of the form of (
2.9
).
To apply the results of the present paper one then needs to prove that the resulting system has the geometric
and regularity properties needed to satisfy Hypotheses
1
–
3
. The above construction, assuming it is successful,
allows one to extend the results on synchronization to the systems as in (
2.38
).
Then, one has to show that the averaging principle proved for the system (
2.9
) implies,
thanks to the existence of
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}
,
an averaging principle for (
2.38
),
more precisely that, writing
F_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)=\rho\,f_{\varphi}(\varphi,\psi)
and starting the evolution at
\varphi_{0}
, with

\psi
distributed according to
m_{0}
, the long time evolution generated by (
2.38
) for small
\rho
is described by
the flow
\Phi^{\prime}_{t}
defined by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\Phi^{\prime}_{t}(\varphi_{0})=f^{\prime}(\Phi^{\prime}_{t}(\varphi_{0})),\qquad\qquad f^{\prime}(\varphi):=\int_{\mathds{T}^{2}}f(\varphi,\psi)\,m_{\varphi}({\rm d}\psi),\\
&\Phi^{\prime}_{0}(\varphi)=\varphi_{0},\phantom{\sum}\end{aligned}\right.\vskip-5.69046pt

(2.40)

where the new measure
m_{\varphi}
can be computed from
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

and, as heuristic arguments suggest, it is expected to
be the SRB measure of the Anosov diffeomorphism
{\mathcal{A}}(\cdot;\varphi)
.

A third possible – and harder – generalization to investigate, already in the skew
product case, is obtained by weakening the hypotheses on the dissipative nature
of the map
{\mathscr{S}}
, for instance by allowing more general interactions where
the rate of contraction of the neutral variable is non-zero only in average,
as in
[
19
,
20
]
, where expanding maps are considered instead of Anosov automorphisms.
For such a kind of interactions,
a fortiori
also in the case of systems of the form (
2.38
)
new phenomena with respect to those found in the present paper
are expected to occur, such as non-uniform hyperbolicity, positive Lyapunov exponents in the central direction,
non-absolutely integrable central direction and metastability.
Very interesting, from a physical point of view, is the conservative case,
where
\langle f(\cdot,\varphi)\rangle
is assumed to vanish for all
\varphi
.
In such a situation, on the basis of the results available for related models
[
17
,
24
,
34
,
44
]
,
one expects the scaling to be
k=\lfloor t/\rho^{2}\rfloor

and to lead in the limit to a stochastic differential equation,
instead of an ordinary differential equation as in the dissipative case.

Finally, it would be of interest to find a more detailed description of the fluctuations
of the process
X_{t}
in (
2.34
) around the flow
\Phi_{t}
in (
2.22
).
By comparison with the results available in the literature
[
36
,
23
,
24
,
21
,
19
,
20
]
,
we expect the stochastic process

\Delta_{t}:=(X_{t}-\Phi_{t})/\sqrt{\rho}

to converge in distribution, as
\rho\to 0^{+}
, to the solution of the stochastic differential equation

\begin{cases}\displaystyle{{\rm d}\Delta_{t}=\partial_{\varphi}\overline{f}(\Phi_{t}(\varphi_{0}))\Delta_{t}{\rm d}t+\sigma(\Phi_{t}(\varphi_{0}))\,{\rm d}B(t)},\phantom{\sum}&\\
\Delta_{0}=0,\phantom{\sum}&\end{cases}\vskip-5.69046pt

(2.41)

where
B(t)
is a standard Brownian motion and

(\sigma(\varphi))^{2}:=\bigl\langle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{f}(\varphi,\cdot)\,\tilde{f}(\varphi,A_{0}^{n}\cdot)\bigr\rangle
,
with
\tilde{f}
defined according to (
2.1
).
Such a result is known to hold with
\sigma>0
in the case in which the chaotic system is
an expanding map, if one requires the function
\varphi\mapsto F(\varphi,\psi,)
not to be cohomologous to a
constant function for any
\psi

[
19
,
20
]
. Since for conservative interactions the scaling
limit is expected to lead to a stochastic differential equation, studying how an equation like (
2.41
) emerges from (
2.9
) in the scaling regime can be seen
as a precursory step before dealing with the more demanding scaling regime for conservative systems.
The fact that
\Delta_{t}
converges to the solution of equation (
2.41
) implies that

W/\sqrt{\rho}
, with
W
(see Subsection
2.3.1
) seen as a random
variable on
\mathds{T}^{2}
, converges in distribution to a normal random variable with
mean 0 and standard deviation
\sigma(0)
, that is

\lim_{\rho\to 0^{+}}m_{0}\left(\{\psi\in\mathds{T}^{2}:W(\psi)\leq\sqrt{\rho}z\}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma(0)^{2}}}\int_{-\infty}^{z}{\rm d}y\,e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2\sigma(0)^{2}}}.\vskip-2.84544pt

(2.42)

To start with, as a consistency check we show how to derive (
2.42
).
First of all, using the notation in Remark
2.7
, with
F=\rho f
and

\mu(\psi):=1-\nu(\psi)
, set

W_{0}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\mu{\circ}A_{0}^{-j}\biggr)\beta{\circ}A_{0}^{-i},\qquad W_{00}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\overline{\mu}^{i-1}\beta{\circ}A_{0}^{-i}.\vskip-5.69046pt

We prove in Subsection
4.3
that
\langle|W-W_{0}|\rangle=O(\rho)
, which
implies that
(W-W_{0})/\sqrt{\rho}
converges in probability to 0,
so that we just need to prove that
W_{0}/\sqrt{\rho}
, in the limit, has the correct normal distribution.
Then, a partial extension of Proposition
4.3
to multi-time correlation functions – whose proof we omit – shows that

\big\langle{\left(W_{0}-W_{00}\right)^{2}}\big\rangle=O(\rho^{2})
.
On the other hand, the central limit theorem for Anosov system
[
15
]
implies
that
W_{00}/\sqrt{\rho}
tends to the correct normal limit as
\rho\to 0^{+}
.
We expect a similar strategy to work for the derivation of (
2.41
), by
proceeding along the lines of Subsection
4.8
.

2.6
Content of the paper and strategy of the proof

The rest of the paper, devoted to the proof of the results stated in Subsections
2.3
and
2.4
,
is organised as follows.

Section
3
contains the proofs of the Theorems
1
to

3
, together with the derivation of the properties of both the
invariant manifold and the conjugation that are needed for dealing with the scaling regime.
The existence of the invariant manifold is formulated as a
fixed-point problem in a suitable Banach space (Theorem
1
).
Thereafter, the conjugation is shown
to admit a series representation which is proved to converge to a
function which satisfies the properties stated in Theorem
2
. This
result implies the existence of the inverse conjugation,
which satisfies similar properties (Corollary
2.12
),
and the mixing properties of the system while evolving toward the invariant manifold (Theorem
3
).
Technically, it turns out to be useful to use the coordinates
(\theta,\psi)
, with
\theta:=\varphi-W(\psi)

and
W(\psi)
as in Subsection
2.3.1
,
in terms of which the dynamics is described by
a map
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}
that we call the
translated map
and the attracting invariant manifold is the flat torus
\mathds{T}^{2}
.

In Section
4
we discuss the averaging problem in the scaling regime:
setting
F(\varphi,\theta)=\rho f(\varphi,\theta)
, the dynamics of
{\mathscr{S}}^{k}(\varphi,\psi)

is studied for
k=\lfloor t/\rho\rfloor
, with
t
fixed and
\rho\to 0^{+}
.
